## AI Research

### [Daycare illnesses](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/byiLDrbj8MNzoHZkL/daycare-illnesses)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Before I had a baby I was pretty agnostic about the idea of daycare. I could imagine various pros and cons but I didn’t have a strong overall opinion. Then I started mentioning the idea to various people. Every parent I spoke to brought up a consideration I hadn’t thought about before—the illnesses.</span></p><p><span>A number of parents, including family members, told me they had sent their baby to daycare only for them to become constantly ill, sometimes severely, until they decided to take them out. This worried me so I asked around some more. Invariably&nbsp;</span><i><span>every single parent&nbsp;</span></i><span>who had tried to send their babies or toddlers to daycare, or who had babies in daycare right now, told me that they were ill more often than not.</span></p><p><span>One mother strongly advised me never to send my baby to daycare. She regretted sending her (normal and healthy) first son to daycare when he was one—he ended up hospitalized with severe pneumonia after a few months of constant illnesses and infections. She told me that after that she didn’t send her other kids to daycare and they had much healthier childhoods.</span></p><p><span>I also started paying more attention to the kids I saw playing outside with their daycare group and noticing that every one had a sniffly nose.</span></p><p><span>I asked on a mothers group chat about people’s experiences with daycare. Again, the same. Some quotes:</span></p><p><span>“They do get sick a lot. I started my son at 2.5 and feel he always has something.”</span></p><p><span>“The limit does not exist.”</span></p><p><span>“brought home every plague (in first 6mo, Covid, HFM, slapcheek, RSV)”</span></p><p><span>“They usually say 8-12 illnesses per year. My girls were sick every 2-3 weeks in their first year of daycare”</span></p><p><span>“My daughter started daycare at 6 months and got sick a ton the first year”</span></p><p><span>Despite all this, many parents who have the option not to (i.e. they can afford in-home care with a nanny or for one parent to stay home) still choose to send their babies and toddlers to daycare. How come? Surely most well-off adults wouldn’t agree to be ill nonstop in exchange for the monetary savings daycare provides?</span></p><p><span>Asking around, it seemed like the most common reason given was that parents believed daycare illnesses “built immunity”; that if their babies and toddlers got sick at daycare they’d get less sick later in childhood and so overall it would net out the same. Unfortunately few could point me to any evidence for this but nevertheless passionately defended the view.</span></p><p><span>The claim that daycare illnesses simply offset childhood and adult illness immediately seemed suspect to me for a number of reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>(Quite confident) The most common illnesses (colds and flu) don’t build immunity in general (in kids or adults) because they mutate every year</span></li><li value="2"><span>(Quite confident) The same illness has a greater risk of complications in babies vs. older children and adults</span></li><li value="3"><span>(Moderately confident) The same illness has a greater duration in babies vs. older children and adults</span></li><li value="4"><span>(Moderately confident) Illness during early development is probably more harmful than illness during adulthood</span></li><li value="5"><span>(Weak guess) Daycare environments are more conducive to disease spread than schools for older kids and the number of possible illnesses is very high; there isn’t just a limited number of things you catch once</span></li></ol><p><span>I&nbsp;</span><a href="https://x.com/NinaPanickssery/status/2038279892073349144?s=20"><span>xeeted</span></a><span>&nbsp;about this:</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!O46q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe49ffb69-1bac-4f37-96e2-e4cdd30bb3e7_1168x294.png" target="_blank"></a></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.6737160120846%;"><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!O46q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe49ffb69-1bac-4f37-96e2-e4cdd30bb3e7_1168x294.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/byiLDrbj8MNzoHZkL/jdptbbk5syacjsxnskrm" /></a></figure><p></p><p><span>A number of people sent me&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.ucl.ac.uk/news/2026/mar/frequent-infections-nursery-help-toddlers-build-immune-systems"><span>this link</span></a><span>, an alleged “study” from UCL showing that “frequent infections in nursery help toddlers build up immune systems”, authored (of course) by a group of parents who all send their kids to nursery (what the British call daycare).</span></p><p><span>The link I was sent was actually a UCL press release summarizing a&nbsp;</span><a href="https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/cmr.00253-25"><span>narrative review paper</span></a><span>&nbsp;and not a study itself. Narrative reviews are susceptible to selection bias because, unlike systematic reviews or meta-analyses, there’s no pre-registered search protocol or&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.prisma-statement.org/"><span>PRISMA</span></a><span>-style methodology requiring them to account for all relevant evidence. But I decided to look into the narrative review more, to assess its validity fairly. I got access to the full publication.</span></p><p><span>Unlike the press release, which ignores these considerations entirely, it&nbsp;</span><i><span>does</span></i><span>&nbsp;engage with severity and age-related vulnerability, conceding that younger toddlers and babies suffer more from the same illnesses. A section on immunology provides a detailed account of why infants under two are more vulnerable—their immune systems are much less effective at fighting the same infections for a plethora of well-understood reasons. The review also cites a large Danish registry study (</span><a href="https://publications.aap.org/pediatrics/article-abstract/118/4/1439/69092/Population-Based-Study-of-the-Impact-of-Childcare"><span>Kamper-Jørgensen et al</span></a><span>) that reports a 69% higher incidence of hospitalization for acute respiratory infections in under-1s in daycare.</span></p><p><span>However, these severity findings are integrated into the review’s conclusions and framing in an incredibly biased way. The introduction describes severe outcomes as occurring “in rare cases,” and the conclusions focus on normalizing the burden and advocating for employer understanding. After establishing the immunological basis for why the same infection is more dangerous in a 6-month-old than a 3-year-old, it doesn’t then ask the hard follow-up question: given this, is the pattern of starting daycare at 6–12 months optimal from a child health perspective? Instead, the review frames this timing as a societal given. The Hand Foot and Mouth Disease section is a good example of the review’s handling: it reports that daycare attendance was associated with more severe cases but then immediately offers mitigating interpretation with no evidence—that prolonged hospital stays might reflect parental work constraints rather than genuine severity.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!no9q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc211b991-ff6c-490c-8372-5858a6a2e45b_1258x1098.png" target="_blank"></a></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 79.00302114803625%;"><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!no9q!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc211b991-ff6c-490c-8372-5858a6a2e45b_1258x1098.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/byiLDrbj8MNzoHZkL/otfrcdphodqnxzrgdt4p" /></a></figure><p></p><p><span>Though the review considers severity, it ignores duration. Their primary metric throughout is episode count. Also, despite discussing a wide variety of pathogens, it doesn’t address which of these infections carry the highest complication rates in infants and toddlers specifically.</span></p><p><span>Finally, the crucial “Illness now or illness later?” is the paper’s weakest portion. It rests on two primary sources for the compensatory immunity claim:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The Tucson Children’s Respiratory Study: a cohort study of ~1,000 American children followed from birth to age 13 in the early 2000s, finding that daycare attendees had more colds at age 2 but fewer by age 6–11.</span></li><li value="2"><span>A Dutch study (Hullegie et al. 2016) of 2220 children followed for 6 years, finding reduced GI illness between ages 2–5 in children with first-year daycare attendance.</span></li></ul><p><span>These are reasonable small studies, but the paper does not cite or engage with the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/ije/article/52/2/466/6839854"><span>Søegaard et al. 2023 study (International Journal of Epidemiology)</span></a><span>—a register-based cohort of over 1 million Danish children followed to age 20, which directly tested and rejected the compensatory immunity hypothesis. Quoting from the study:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>We observed 4 599 993 independent episodes of infection (antimicrobial exposure) during follow-up. Childcare enrolment transiently increased infection rates; the younger the child, the greater the increase. The resulting increased cumulative number of infections associated with earlier age at childcare enrolment was not compensated by lower infection risk later in childhood or adolescence.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is arguably the single most relevant study for the paper’s central “illness now or illness later” question, and it’s three orders of magnitude larger than either study the authors cite. Its absence is hard to explain—it was published in a top epidemiology journal in late 2022 (available online November 2022), well before the review was written.</span></p><p><span>Accordingly, they hedge their conclusions carefully—“attendance at formal childcare may tip the balance in favor of infection now rather than later”, but their press release ignores any nuance, referring to daycare as an “immune boot camp”.</span></p><p><span>So overall, the compensatory immunity claim seems very weak and my prior that daycare illness is straight-up bad remains. Parents are citing biased reviews from motivated researchers. We are only&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w29524/w29524.pdf"><span>beginning to understand the deleterious effects of increased viral load in infants</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sdIw!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa2c2a4aa-a501-4cd5-a3ac-80014b01c740_1168x1210.png" target="_blank"></a></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 72.20543806646525%;"><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sdIw!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fa2c2a4aa-a501-4cd5-a3ac-80014b01c740_1168x1210.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/byiLDrbj8MNzoHZkL/mxy5dxqmzhfg9guft3ne" /></a></figure><p></p><p><span>I predict that in the future we’ll learn more about the side-effects of increased viral load on intelligence, wellbeing, fatigue etc. The “just the sniffles” mentality is a harmful attitude toward infections that promotes the dismissal of phenomena that substantially impact child and adult wellbeing.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/byiLDrbj8MNzoHZkL/daycare-illnesses#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Morale](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/53ZAzbdzGJHGeE5rs/morale)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>One particularly pernicious condition is low morale. Morale is, roughly, "the belief that if you work hard, your conditions will improve." If your morale is low, you can't push through adversity. It's also very easy to accidentally drop your morale through standard rationalist life-optimization.</span></p><p><span>It's easy to optimize for wellbeing and miss out on the factors which affect morale, especially if you're working on something important, like not having everyone die. One example is working at an office that feeds you three meals per day. This seems optimal: eating is nice, and cooking is effort. Obvious choice.</span></p><h3><span>Example</span></h3><p><span>But morale doesn't come from having nice things. Consider a rich teenager. He gets basically every material need satisfied: maids clean, chefs cook, his family takes him on holiday four times a year. What happens when this kid comes up against something really difficult in school? He probably doesn't push through.</span></p><p><span>"Aha", I hear you say. "That kid has never faced adversity. Of course he's not going to handle it well." Ok, suppose he gets kicked in the shins every day and called a </span><i><span>posh twat</span></i><span> by some local youths, but still goes into school. That's adversity, will that work? Will he have higher morale now? I don't think so.</span></p><p><span>Now, what about if he plays the cello in the school orchestra. Or he plays for the school football team. I think that might work, even if he's not the best kid in the school at either of those things. It's not about having nice things or having bad things, it's about something else</span></p><h2><span>II </span></h2><p><span>Morale comes from </span><i><span>having the nice things in your life correlated with effort</span></i><span>. Cooking your own dinner is basically microdosing returns to investing effort: if you put in effort, you eat steak frites with peppercorn sauce. If you don't, you get eat chicken and rice. </span></p><p><span>It doesn't have to be cooking, basically any hobby works like this, as long as you get returns to effort. It might be art, or weightlifting, or whatever. You just need to keep reminding your brain that effort has a purpose.</span></p><p><span>This is especially important when you work in an area (like not having everyone die) where the returns on effort are hard to come by. Good software engineering looks like solving a PR in a day or so (or whatever you people do). Good alignment research might mean chasing a concept for weeks, only to have it fail.</span></p><p><span>The early stages of dating can also induce low morale. Sometimes, things just fall apart due to random incompatibilities which aren't your fault. Long-term relationships are much less like this: you can just do things (plan dates with your partner and enjoy their company). </span></p><p><span>John Wentworth has written about a minor depression presenting as extremely low morale amongst rationalist types. I don't think you should wait until it gets that bad before you improve your morale. I think you should think about it now.</span></p><h2><span>III</span></h2><p><span>Morale doesn't just matter on an individual level, it also matters on the scale of whole societies. In this case, it doesn't just matter whether an individual gets rewarded for effort, it matters whether they see others rewarded for effort---and whether or not they see others punished for a lack of effort.</span></p><p><span>It's a truism that the most effective way to kill morale is to reward lazy or incompetent employees. You can do one better if you reward active sabotage. The harm of small but visible crimes (like fare-dodging on public transport) is, in part, the damage to the morale of everyone around.</span></p><p><span>There should be a hack for societal morale, though, and it's economic growth. People generally put some amount of effort into their work. If they can afford a better car each year, they'll attribute that to their own grit, and not an increase in the productivity of a Chinese factory.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, there's a twist in the twist. People are really awful at understanding nominal inflation. If the price goes up a bit (even if their wages more than match it) the price increase </span><i><span>just feels</span></i><span> like a random, unfair, morale-reducing loss. I conjecture this is a big contributor to the American Vibecession. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/53ZAzbdzGJHGeE5rs/morale#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Eggs, rooms, puzzles, and talking about AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/eggs-rooms-puzzles-and-talking-about-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>I live with five friends in a big house, and two things I’ve done in it on this particular Sunday are hide 156 easter eggs all around, and reach a tentative joint decision on the allocation of four of its rooms.</p>

<p>These tasks are delightful to me for a reason they have in common, and from which I hope to gesture at extremely far reaching conclusions.</p>

<h2>Easter eggs</h2>

<p>A room usually seems like a simple thing to me—a big box, with some smaller mostly boxish objects and holes in it. Each of those things also usually seems simple: a cupboard is a box-shaped hole, with a movable thin-box-shaped front, which has hinges (the most complicated part, but in this picture their only qualities are letting flat surfaces rotate around fixed edges). Sometimes a cupboard has shelves, which are like planes breaking up the space.</p>

<p>In this picture, hiding easter eggs well is hard! Like, I could put one in the cupboard? On the top shelf? Or the bottom shelf! They’ll never find it there!</p>

<p>These are not good hiding places.</p>

<p>In order to hide easter eggs well, you need to see a lot of detail that you were abstracting away in the simple picture. The weird ridge along the back of the cupboard, or a wire looping under a lip around the front, or brackets holding up the shelves that have spaces in them where something could be wedged, or a rogue curl of onion peel in a back corner.</p>

<p>Here is one of my favorite hiding spots—can you see the egg?</p>

<p><img alt="cushion with hidden egg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/pci0nokwuziauhq2r9fq" /></p>

<p>Answer below:</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p>.</p>

<p><img alt="hidden egg up closer" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/tzkqxvienp0efg3x9yoo" /></p>

<p><img alt="hidden egg very close" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/wrwxfmgzfoqwujpmrj7v" /></p>

<p>I like it because a cushion so much seems like an inflated square in my mind—yes, with some sort of pattern, and perhaps somewhat worn out, but I don’t expect a pattern + worn out = you can hide a substantial solid object on the surface of it.</p>

<p>Here is an especially empty room (one of the ones in need of allocation), currently known as ‘the puzzle room’:</p>

<p><img alt="puzzle room" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/k2wbwygvrmk4twv1huw9" /></p>

<p>I hid ten eggs in it (probably two visible in this picture), and it took a while for people to find them all, which seemed to aggressively help some of the egg-seekers receive a similar experience of space containing details that are somehow really hard to see even if you try.</p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/omh8qglq2dntudtfmt3c" /></p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/u7afpmuob0vwfucog9ku" /></p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/bcfvtsygpylb7uiiyojr" /></p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/lqmrwrtxfug10o62kqto" /></p>

<p>It would be one thing to have a kind of ‘level of detail dial’ that you could read and consciously turn up and down the level as you see fit. But an interesting thing about watching people search for easter eggs is that they can’t necessarily choose which things they are abstracting out, or fully tell how ‘carefully’ they are looking. You can put eggs in plain sight of them, and they think they are looking carefully, but just don’t see the egg. By the time a person has perceived anything at all, they have simplified it. You can’t just look at all the raw detail, and check it for eggs.</p>

<p>Besides not being able to control which abstractions you use, it seems to me now that an adversary (such as an egg-hider) can guess and exploit your habits of abstracting. Among the details of the cupboard, even if you are looking carefully at the shape of the sides, you might still miss the onion peel, because it’s random dirt, and you are examining the cupboard. That’s another nice thing about the ragged cushion—if you habitually round off worn-out things to what they are meant to be, it’s hard to see the detail of how it is falling apart, and thus the egg.</p>

<p>In another possible example, one of our bathrooms has a ‘bathroom!’ label on it, which I expect my housemates are used to seeing and ignoring, and visitors perhaps also tune out on their way to look for eggs inside what they have already determined to be a bathroom. I put an egg behind it, held by the super-post-it-note glue, which was a pretty unsubtle disruption to the smoothness of the sign, but this egg wasn’t found until it was accidentally knocked out at the very end.</p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/spicl5wr3ozskasivqji" /></p>

<h2>Rooms</h2>

<p>Allocating rooms seems like it should be a simple thing—there are only a few options! Like, if you have four rooms, and Alice and Bob each basically need a place to sleep and to work, then it seems like you should be able to consider the 24 possibilities and be done. But actually (at least in houses I live in) what exact spaces are the ‘rooms’ in question is often more ambiguous than you might think, and what set of activities will be expected or people will be owners also contains many more possibilities than I see at first.</p>

<p>I’m more confused about how this happens with rooms, but I have twice in this house had the experience of mulling over such a question for what seems like unreasonably long, and coming up with new ideas we hadn’t thought of or taken seriously, and ending up with a satisfactory arrangement. This time, our tentative plan involves one of the bedrooms also being a recording studio, and there being three total rooms with beds in among two people. Which all feels very simple in retrospect, but I have been haplessly ideating about this for weeks.</p>

<p>It again feels kind of magical and wholesome to stare at the simple things long enough and well enough to see them more richly, in ways that you couldn’t just choose to, and for this to solve your problem.</p>

<h2>Classic puzzles</h2>

<p>This kind of situation - an abstraction you take for granted that makes a problem hard, and gaps in the abstraction that let you do better, is a classic way to construct a puzzle. For instance (from<a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/theydidthemath/comments/175njzc/request_what_is_the_biggest_number_that_can_be/"> Reddit</a>):</p>

<p><img alt="alt_text" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/cakqfaukjnv0njbmjt6k" /></p>

<h2>AI risk</h2>

<p>A thing that has annoyed me for a long time in talking to people about AI risk is that they often do it in very abstract terms—”we need safety progress relative to capabilities progress”, or “such and such will get a decisive strategic advantage and there will be value lockin”—and then expect to be correct, like pretty confidently!</p>

<p>I love abstractions quite a lot compared to most people (I once scored 100% on the relevant axis of the Myers-Briggs test!) but I’m also expecting abstractions to have relevant frayed edges all over the place. And this is particularly relevant if you are trying to solve problems and are struggling to see solutions.</p>

<p>In particular, for instance, I often hear that it is pointless or silly to try not to build really dangerous AI technology because “it’s a race”. But before you give up on preventing this disaster, I really want you to spend at least as much attention seeing the details of the world below the level of “arms race” than my boyfriend spent peering at our laundry machines before he found the egg there.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3rnM9eDTx4hHSbfER/eggs-rooms-puzzles-and-talking-about-ai#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Blast Radius Principle](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rDzMraAEkBBewn7vy/the-blast-radius-principle)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Decentralize or Die.</span></i></p><p><span>In April 2024, a salvo of cruise missiles destroyed the Trypilska thermal power plant, the largest in the Kyiv region, in under an hour. In June 2023, the destruction of the Kakhovka dam left a million people without drinking water and wiped out an entire irrigation system downstream. Throughout three winters, strikes on combined heat and power plants have left apartment buildings in Kyiv at indoor temperatures barely above freezing. In December 2023, a single cyberattack on Kyivstar, Ukraine's largest mobile operator, cut phone and internet service for millions.</span></p><p><span>One would think that under such attacks on infrastructure any society must necessarily collapse. Or at least that’s what Putin hopes for. But the last time I’ve checked, Ukraine was still very much alive and kicking. The question is: how is that possible?</span></p><p><span>***</span></p><p><span>In winter 2022, when the blackout in Kyiv happened for the first time, people had to cope for themselves. Here’s Tymofiy Mylovanov, professor at Kyiv School of Economics, </span><a href="https://x.com/Mylovanov/status/1595661954798026752"><span>tweeting</span></a><span> in real-time:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>There is no electricity, no heating, no water. Outside temperature is around freezing. The apartment is still warm from the previous days. We will see how long it lasts. We have blankets, sleeping bags, warm clothes. I am not too worried about heating until temperature goes below -10 C / 14 F. But the water is another issue. The problem is toilets. We have stockpiled about 100 litters of water. There is also snow on our balcony. It is a surprisingly large supply of water. But every time I go there to get it, I have to let the cold air in; not good. For now, the cell network is up, although the quality varies. Thus, I have internet. Internet is critical for food. Yesterday we went to a grocery store to buy up a bit more stuff in case there will be shortages. Food is there, no lines. The challenge is to pay. Most registers work with cash only. Just a few are connected to accept credit cards. Through cell network. The banking system is stable, but I will go get some cash in case Telekom or banks go down Our stove is electric. This means no warm food until the electricity is back. This is not fun. We have to fix it. There are two parts to our plan. First, we will buy an equivalent of a home Tesla battery. So it can be charged when there is electricity. This will also solve, somewhat, the heating problem, as we have already bought some electric heaters. But the electricity might be off for a long time and so we need gas or wood cooking equipment. I guess we have to go shopping. Stores work. They run huge diesel generators.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Later that day he dryly </span><a href="https://x.com/Mylovanov/status/1595886261595787271"><span>comments</span></a><span>: “In the morning I said I was not worried about heating. Instead, I was concerned about water and sanitation. Boy, was I wrong.”</span></p><p><span>It’s worth reading the tweets from the next few days: Getting a generator, setting it up, placing it on balcony so that fumes stay outside, getting the wires in without letting the cold in as well. Go </span><a href="https://x.com/Mylovanov/status/1596050892327129090"><span>check it out</span></a><span> for yourself.</span></p><p><span>Anyway, what followed was a series of adaptations, a kind of military vs. civilian arms race. Through the first winter, the strategy was simply to repair what Russia destroyed. Substations and transformers that could be replaced within weeks with donated European spares.</span></p><p><span>In the meantime, for the millions of affected people, the government created stopgaps. Over 10,000 heated public spaces in schools, government buildings, and railway stations offered electricity, water, internet, and phone charging. Kyiv deployed mobile boiler houses that could run for days without refueling. Hospitals installed Tesla Powerwalls. Cafes ran diesel generators and became de facto community centers.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.teploproukrajinu.cz/en/" target="_blank"><img alt="Mobilní kotelna v lodním kontejneru" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rDzMraAEkBBewn7vy/drzcvc0cqgdvy6rrpwzy" /></a></p><p><span>Mobile boiler house in a shipping container. You truck one in, connect it to a building's existing heating pipes, and it starts working.</span></p><p><span>I’ve donated to some of those efforts, maybe you did too. And taken all together, it worked. Kind of. But by 2024 Russia adapted. Strikes shifted from repairable transmission equipment to the power plants themselves, assets that take years to rebuild. The Trypilska plant was partially restored after its destruction, then it was struck again by drones months later. And again after that. With two-thirds of generation capacity gone and every thermal plant in the country damaged, it became clear that restoring the old centralized system was not a viable strategy.</span></p><p><span>Ukraine's response shifted. It was not to rebuild what was destroyed but to replace it with something less centralized. Something too dispersed to target. Instead of restoring the Trypilska plant's 1,800 megawatts, hundreds of small </span><a href="https://reinvantage.org/ukraines-decentralised-energy-solution/"><span>cogeneration units</span></a><span> were scattered across the region, compact gas turbines producing 5 to 40 megawatts each, generating heat alongside the electricity. By late 2025, Ukraine's heating sector alone ran over 180 such units as well as hundreds of modular boilers. Hospitals, water utilities, and apartment blocks are organized into autonomous energy islands, microgrids that keep functioning even if the national grid goes dark. No single unit is worth a cruise missile. And a destroyed module can be replaced with a phone call and a truck from Poland.</span></p><p><span>The same logic extends to water. Ukraine's centralized water systems are inherited from the Soviet era. A single pumping station serves hundreds of thousands of people. They are just as vulnerable as the power plants. Strikes on the grid cut electricity to pumps. Without pumps, water stops flowing. In winter, standing water in pipes freezes and bursts them, cascading damage across entire districts.</span></p><p><span>In Mykolaiv, a damaged pipeline to the Dnipro River left 300,000 residents relying on salty, barely drinkable water from a local estuary for over a year. The </span><a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/striving-access-security-and-sustainability"><span>response</span></a><span> mirrors the energy transformation: water utilities are installing their own solar panels and battery storage to decouple from the grid entirely.</span></p><p><span>Solar panels are, under these circumstances, close to an ideal solution. They are cheap, manufactured at scale, and can be replaced in a single day. By early 2024, Ukrainian households and businesses had installed nearly </span><a href="https://www.iea.org/reports/ukraines-energy-security-and-the-coming-winter/ukraines-energy-system-under-attack"><span>1,500 megawatts</span></a><span> of rooftop solar. Not because of climate change, but because of survival. Solar panels are inherently dispersed. There is no single set of coordinates an attacker can hit to disable them all. And destroying them one by one would cost the attacker more in munitions than the panels are worth.</span></p><p><span>This kind of arithmetic pops up everywhere. In the ongoing Iran war, Ukrainian military observers were flabbergasted by Gulf states and the US burning through hundreds of </span><a href="https://www.cfr.org/articles/the-new-era-of-drone-warfare-takes-root-in-iran"><span>Patriot missiles, $4 million each, to shoot down cheap Iranian Shaheed drones, $35,000 apiece</span></a><span>. If destroying a target costs more than the target itself, the attacker loses even if the strike succeeds.</span></p><p><span>A different kind of decentralization is happening in the telecommunications domain. The infrastructure was already </span><a href="https://www.chathamhouse.org/2024/08/internet-under-attack/04-internet-resilience-ukraine"><span>fairly decentralized</span></a><span> to start with, a legacy of makeshift internet adoption that happened in many Ostblock countries, with many small ISPs emerging independently. The war pushed this further. Ukraine has adopted a </span><a href="https://ts2.tech/en/telecommunications-infrastructure-in-ukraine-2022-2025-destruction-and-resilience/"><span>layered backup approach</span></a><span>: if fiber broadband fails, mobile networks fill the gap; if mobile networks are knocked out, Starlink steps in as a last resort.</span></p><p><span>The logic extends to </span><a href="https://www.brookings.edu/articles/ukraine-digital-government-is-central-to-resilience/"><span>government services</span></a><span>. There’s the Trembita data exchange platform, where government services talk each other directly without centralizing the data. (Trembita is based on Estonian X-Road system — the birth of Estonian e-gov technology is a fascinating story in itself, and there’s a </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Rebooting-Nation-Incredible-Government-Revolution/dp/1805263013"><span>whole book</span></a><span> about it!) Built on top of it, there’s the Diia app that allows citizens to file taxes, register vehicles, access medical records, open bank accounts, register births, and start businesses, all from a smartphone. This, of course, means there’s no single office building to target so as to disrupt a particular kind of activity.</span></p><p><span>Add to that Ukrainian governmental data are now stored in the cloud. A week before the invasion, Ukraine's parliament quietly amended a law that had required government data to be stored physically in Ukraine. On the day the missiles started flying, the Ukrainian ambassador in London met AWS engineers and decided to </span><a href="https://www.aboutamazon.eu/news/aws/safeguarding-ukraines-data-to-preserve-its-present-and-build-its-future"><span>fly three AWS Snowballs</span></a><span>, hardened suitcases that hold 80 terabytes each, from Dublin to Poland and then move them to Ukraine the very next day. Ukrainian technicians copied population registers, land ownership records, and tax databases onto them and shipped them back out.</span></p><p><span>It was a race. On the day of the invasion, cruise missiles struck government server facilities while Russian cyber operatives simultaneously deployed wiper malware, software designed to permanently destroy data, against hundreds of Ukrainian government systems. Some data was lost, but the most critical registries were already gone, smuggled out of the country in carry-on luggage.</span></p><p><span>***</span><br /><span>On the battlefield, where all these trends are even more severe, concentration has become suicidal. Russian infantry now advances in groups of two or three. Anything larger is an invitation for a drone strike. Warships are floating targets. Russia's Black Sea Fleet retreated from Crimea after losing vessels to cheap unmanned boats. In the </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zsBLtEKBQ2Q"><span>Hedgehog 2025</span></a><span> exercise in Estonia, a small team of Ukrainians and Estonians with drones, acting as the opposing force, wiped out two NATO battalions, thousands of soldiers, in half a day, not least because they had moved in columns, parked their vehicles in close formations and failed to scatter under attack.</span></p><p><span>They made the same mistake as the designers of Soviet-era power grids: they concentrated value and got destroyed for it. Call it the blast radius principle. In a war of attrition, any asset whose destruction is worth more than the cost of the weapon that can reach it will, sooner or later, be destroyed. The only effective strategy is to push the value of each individual target below that threshold, to become, in effect, too small to bomb.</span></p><p><span>When Rheinmetall’s CEO recently made a </span><a href="https://www.france24.com/en/live-news/20260329-rheinmetall-addresses-row-over-ceo-s-ukraine-housewives-comment"><span>condescending comment</span></a><span> about Ukrainian housewives 3D-printing drones in their kitchens, much merriment ensued. Because Rheinmetall, of course, builds the very kind of heavy conventional, WWII-style hardware that the developments in Ukraine are rapidly making obsolete.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!CyD1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fe4c62893-bd38-478e-a8ca-34dc969dc2f0_1024x1024.jpeg" target="_blank"><img alt="Ukrainian 'housewives' defending Europe, after German CEO's gaffe (Ukraine  Battlefield update, Day 1,496) – EUobserver" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rDzMraAEkBBewn7vy/wjkotd2yyk0tz1ztvtsx" /></a></p><p><span>But mockery aside for a moment: if you’ve spent any time around progress studies, the phrase “housewives building drones in kitchens” makes you prick up your ears. It triggers a specific association: cottage industry, the small-scale, home-based production that preceded and enabled the industrial revolution. It makes you think about how the modes of production change over centuries.</span></p><p><span>You know that kings and generals don’t make history. One empire falls, another rises, nothing fundamentally changes. What does matter is new technology. Even more so new technology which fundamentally changes how things are done. Technology that reshapes the economics of entire production chains. Agriculture. Road system. Bill of exchange. Putting-out manufacture. Joint-stock company. Assembly line. The humble shipping container…</span></p><p><span>Does decentralization, as seen in Ukraine, fit the bill? We don’t know. FirePoint, the Ukrainian company producing the much-spoken-about FP drones, is </span><a href="https://united24media.com/latest-news/ukraine-is-producing-200-long-range-strike-drones-a-day-and-says-output-can-triple-16652"><span>distributed</span></a><span> across more than 50 manufacturing sites throughout the country. But that’s nothing new. The allied bombing campaign during WWII failed to halt German aircraft manufacture precisely because Germany had decentralized its industries. Albert Speer, then the minister of armaments, dispersed production into hundreds of small workshops, caves, tunnels, and forest sites across the Reich. German aircraft production actually increased in 1944, the year of the heaviest bombing. But then, after the war, German industry did concentrate again.</span></p><p><span>What seems different this time, though, is the spillover into the civilian sector. Speer dispersed munitions factories, but German civilians kept heating their homes the same way throughout the war. In Ukraine, the dispersal extends to utilities, water systems, telecommunications, government services. Russians bomb a heating plant, the heating network disperses into dozens of autonomous microgrids.</span></p><p><span>The obvious objection is that this is a wartime hack, not a permanent transformation. Distributed systems sacrifice economies of scale. A hundred small gas turbines are less efficient than one large power plant. Once the war ends and the skies are safe, the economic logic will reassert itself and everything will concentrate again.</span></p><p><span>And indeed, in some cases, that's exactly what will happen. Ukraine is currently bombing Russian oil refineries and fertilizer plants, and although cracking crude oil in plastic bottles in a kitchen is exactly the sort of thing you might expect Eastern Europeans to do, it's unlikely to match the efficiency of a proper refinery. Some industries have genuinely irreducible physical economies of scale. The chemistry demands large reaction vessels, the thermodynamics reward concentration. Similarly, some infrastructure simply cannot be distributed. It's hard to imagine a decentralized railway system or a dispersed deep-water port — at least short of giving up on it and transporting everything by drone.</span></p><p><span>But not all economies of scale require spatial proximity. Sometimes, it’s just sheer scale that matters, not necessarily the co-location. Case in point: solar panels. Other times the crucial element is the organizational structure, not the physical location of the employees. Basically any service offered over internet is like that.</span></p><p><span>But all that being said, there’s a specific reason to think some of these changes may stick.</span></p><p><span>Over the past fifty years we’ve accumulated an entire arsenal of distributed technologies. Packet-switched networks. Drones. Solar panels. Distributed databases. 3D printing. Even nerdy cypherpunk inventions like public key cryptography, zero-knowledge proofs and cryptographic ledgers. And it’s not just technical stuff. We’ve developed distributed social technologies too: open-source-style cooperation (who would have predicted that military intelligence, of all things, would be the next domain to go open-source?), </span><a href="https://www.250bpm.com/p/the-ukraine-war-and-the-kill-market"><span>market design</span></a><span>, remote work, video conferencing. Even prediction markets as a tool for aggregating dispersed knowledge.</span></p><p><span>Some of these are already ubiquitous. Around 70% of the world’s population already has access to the Internet, a network famously designed to route around damage during a nuclear war. But others feel like we’re barely scratching the surface. 3D printing has existed for decades, yet it still feels like a technology that we are only playing with. We may be like pre-Columbian Americans, whose children played with wheeled toys, but the adults carried loads on their backs.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mhye!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F268b17b1-6458-49b3-9310-95e07dab6e06_537x503.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rDzMraAEkBBewn7vy/cpixbrtplojqjjebjap2" /></a></p><p><span>Mesoamerican wheeled toy.</span></p><p><span>Based on historical examples, we know that inventing a technology is often not the bottleneck. The aeolipile was invented in the first century AD, but we still had to wait another seventeen centuries to get an actual steam engine. Gutenberg </span><a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/ada-palmer"><span>went bankrupt</span></a><span>. Adopting a technology is dependent on complex interplay of socio-economic forces that, at a certain moment, make the technology so desirable that people start using it despite all the drawbacks and overcoming all the vested interests. Then the learning curves kick in.</span></p><p><span>Two questions remain. Are those distributed technologies already adequately exploited, or are they like dead wood lying around in a forest, waiting for a spark? And if the latter is true, are the incentives created by the war in Ukraine — or for that matter, by similar future war elsewhere — sufficient to ignite it? They may be. Because once the enemy starts bombing companies, the incentives change. Working from home ceases to be a nice perk. Suddenly, it’s either work from home or die.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rDzMraAEkBBewn7vy/the-blast-radius-principle#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Your Mom is a Chimera](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cHwWcoaqCHhuHAiz5/your-mom-is-a-chimera)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775945832/lexical_client_uploads/pq6gr0pjnqbth4abip6y.png" /><p><span>And so are you!</span></p><p><span>When you were a fetus, you were sending millions of your cells through the placenta into your mom. And she was sending her cells into you, although to a lesser degree. These cells made themselves right at home, differentiating into heart, blood, and even brain cells. This phenomenon is called </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8762399" rel="noreferrer"><span>feto-maternal microchimerism</span></a><span>, and is one of the wildest things in placental mammal pregnancy.</span></p><h2><span>What?</span></h2><p><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6714269" rel="noreferrer"><span>Microchimerism</span></a><span> is generally defined as the presence of a small population of genetically distinct cells in an organism. When the fetus sends cells to the mother it's called Fetal Microchimerism (FMc) and when the mother sends cells to the fetus it's called Maternal Microchimerism (MMc). The actual cells are fetal microchimeric cells (FMC) and maternal microchimeric cells (MMC).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775963366/lexical_client_uploads/urnk8hjca4g8mzv6s7fy.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>yes somehow this is the official </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8762399/" rel="noreferrer"><span>pubmed diagram</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>You may have heard that the </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4305167/" rel="noreferrer"><span>placenta </span></a><span>is an organ that provides oxygen and nutrients to the fetus during development. Which is true. What's less commonly known is that the placenta also does bidirectional cell and genetic material trafficking, similar to drugs and humans across the US-Mexico border. </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6714269" rel="noreferrer"></a><span>Most of these cells are </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15251948/" rel="noreferrer"><span>quickly killed</span></a><span> by the mother's immune system immediately after to two weeks after birth, but some have been found in people's brains after </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3654737/" rel="noreferrer"><span>three decades</span></a><span>. How does a fetus cell cross the blood brain barrier and become a brain cell? No one knows! It's also an open question if these "brain cells" can functionally integrate with the mom's brain circuits and process neuronal activations: They merely "</span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/stmcls/article-abstract/23/10/1443/6399855" rel="noreferrer"><span>adopt</span></a><span> locations, morphologies, and expression of immunocytochemical markers" of host neurons but further research is needed to determine if they have physiological significance. Weird!</span></p><h2><span>But Why?</span></h2><p><span>There are many theories but no one knows for sure why it happens, what these fetal sleeper cells are up to, or if it's net </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7543167/" rel="noreferrer"><span>negative/positive</span></a><span> for the mom.  </span></p><h3><span>Pros</span></h3><p><span>There is evidence FMCs can help with </span><a href="http://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4199806/" rel="noreferrer"><span>maternal wound healing</span></a><span>. Unlike adults, human fetuses before the second trimester can </span><a href="https://storage.imrpress.com/imr/journal/FBL/article/492008/1752765801663.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>regenerate wounds without scarring</span></a><span>. Fetal cells have been observed homing into and gathering in damaged tissues of </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1534580722003756#bib20" rel="noreferrer"><span>almost all maternal organs</span></a><span>. For example, a study (n=1230) found that patients with peripartum cardiomyopathy (heart dysfunction occurring in late pregnancy or shortly after birth) had a roughly </span><a href="https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM200004133421502" rel="noreferrer"><span>70% lower risk of death</span></a><span> than general cardiomyopathy patients (you can literally fix your mom's broken heart!). FMCs have also been found in skin wounds such as </span><a href="https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/epdf/10.4161/chim.28746?src=getftr&amp;utm_source=sciencedirect_contenthosting&amp;getft_integrator=sciencedirect_contenthosting" rel="noreferrer"><span>C-section scars</span></a><span>. They figured this out by using Fluorescent In Situ Hybridization (FISH) to stain X chromosomes red and Y chromosomes green. In the picture below, the blue circles are nuclei and the arrows point to the ones with both X and Y chromosomes (male fetus DNA in a woman!)</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1775959677/lexical_client_uploads/pnpiyqnmywepoauakrou.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24717775/#&amp;gid=article-figures&amp;pid=captionless-figure-uid-0" rel="noreferrer"><span>Source</span></a><span> </span></p></figcaption></figure><h3><span>Cons</span></h3><p><span>All sorts of fun things like </span><a href="https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/full/10.1161/HYPERTENSIONAHA.113.01486" rel="noreferrer"><span>preeclampsia</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(05)60395-9/abstract" rel="noreferrer"><span>spontaneous preterm labor</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.pnas.org/doi/abs/10.1073/pnas.1904779116" rel="noreferrer"><span>rheumatoid arthritis</span></a><span> and other </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29087650/" rel="noreferrer"><span>autoimmune disorders.</span></a><span> FMCs also </span><a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/bies.201500059" rel="noreferrer"><span>increase transfer of resources</span></a><span> to the fetus (a con for the mom but pro for the fetus). They route more nutrients through the placenta during the pregnancy, and cause physiological changes in the mother after birth (e.g. in lactation, thermoregulation, and attachment systems). As a fetus, you are kind of at war with your mom. You would totally want her to spend limitless resources on only you, but she might want to not do that and prioritize her own survival and potential future offspring. </span></p><h2><span>What do I do with this information?</span></h2><p><span>Call your mom</span></p><h2><span>Conclusion</span></h2><p><span>When we were kids my mom would often tell my brother and I we were her 心肝 (Xīngān, heart and liver). This might sound weird but it's a common Chinese term for endearment, like "honey" or "darling" in English. It's interesting to learn now that this is also sort of literally true. </span></p><p><span>Mothers and their children often have a special bond, and it might be cool to know a part of you is always in your mom, and a part of her is always in you (unless you didn't have a good relationship, in which case don't think about it). </span></p><p><span>There are many open questions in this exciting research field. In the future, perhaps we can study these FMCs and learn how they do their thing, making better organ transplants, stem cell therapies, hybrid animals, or brain replacement for eventual digital uploading. </span></p><p><i><span>Yo momma so fat she has room for two humans worth of allogeneic DNA in her neurons, bone marrow, and organs </span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cHwWcoaqCHhuHAiz5/your-mom-is-a-chimera#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [How to make good tea](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d4SNYCop34nX2o6ay/how-to-make-good-tea)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>If you're starting from a baseline of drinking </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NvGGp3ASHXtt7xXdZ/why-is-american-mass-market-tea-so-terrible" rel="noreferrer"><span>relatively cheap mass-market teabags</span></a><span>, the easiest way to marginally improve your tea quality is by making sure you don't oversteep it.  If it's a typical black tea, try 3 minutes (rather than 4-5).  If it's some kind of green, try 2 minutes, and you also have a second very easy marginal improvement: use colder water.  Most greens should be brewed at 175F/80C.  If you don't have an adjustable-temperature tea kettle, you can get pretty close by pouring boiling water into a mug and then waiting 2-3 minutes.</span></p><p><span>But let's say you actually want to drink good tea, rather than marginal improvements on bad tea.</span></p><p><span>This post will only be covering western style brewing, which is the brewing style familiar to most westerners: one long steep, 300-400ml of water, 2-3g of tea.</span></p><h2><span>Equipment</span></h2><p><span>Your hard requirements are high-quality loose leaf tea, a </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Precision-Digital-Jewelry-Backlit-Stainless/dp/B07DJBDL6L" rel="noreferrer"><span>tea scale</span></a><span> with precision to at least 0.1 grams, and an </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/OXO-BREW-Tea-Infuser-Basket/dp/B07GF4WYGT" rel="noreferrer"><span>infuser</span></a><span>.  (If you're brewing for other people, rather than just yourself, you also want a </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Hiware-Removable-Infuser-Stovetop-Blooming/dp/B01LZSA84J" rel="noreferrer"><span>tea pot with its own infuser</span></a><span>.)  You don't want to be eyeballing quantities of loose leaf tea without a bunch of practice, and you also don't want to be stuck fishing tea leaves out of your tea</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd59n5zyvix5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd59n5zyvix5">[1]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Despite my pretensions otherwise, you can in fact get away with using boiling water for pretty much any kind of tea, except most greens (and yellows).  Many oolongs will take somewhat better to e.g. 190F, but boiling water will rarely be catastrophic.  So while I think you should get an adjustable-temperature tea kettle</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpthqnmxb85e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpthqnmxb85e">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, it's probably not your number one priority.</span></p><h2><span>How to find teas that you like</span></h2><p><span>The best way to figure out what you like is to try a bunch of different things.  I never shop at local tea stores because their selections are often limited and they tend to be more expensive than online vendors, holding quality constant.  However, local tea stores can be great if you're just getting into tea, because they allow you to iterate quickly.  It would suck to order a few different tea samples online, wait three weeks for them to ship from China, and then discover that you don't like any of them.  Here are a couple of heuristics for which local tea store to pick, if you have more than one option:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It primarily sells unflavored, unadulterated loose leaf tea.  If a large percentage of their teas are "blends" or have inclusions like fruits, flowers, herbs, etc, this is a sign that they are less focused on sourcing and selling the kinds of teas that I would find interesting</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefljkeu94glsb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnljkeu94glsb">[3]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It sells tea in small enough quantities that it doesn't cost you an arm and a leg just to try a tea.  5-10 grams is ideal; 20-30 grams is often still tolerable, price-wise</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrjisk6asgqi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrjisk6asgqi">[4]</a></sup></span><span>.  Some places will also serve you a flight of teas in-store; this can be a pretty efficient way to try a few - but mind your caffeine consumption.  However, you will in all likelihood need to make peace with buying tea samples and then giving the rest away after trying them once (or letting them collect dust on your shelves).  Life is too short to drink </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tyMdPwd8x2RygcheE/sunk-cost-fallacy" rel="noreferrer"><span>sunk cost fallacy</span></a><span> tea.</span></li></ul><p><span>Online vendors based in the US are a bit of a middle ground - their shipping speeds, breadth of selection, and prices will often be somewhere in between your local brick-and-mortar and online Chinese vendors.  Some of them even explicitly operate as resellers for vendors from other countries</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8ywoavlb2cc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8ywoavlb2cc">[5]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><h3><span>A Digression on Variety</span></h3><p><span>The amount of variety in tea is enormous.  In the past 4 years, I've tried on the order of 40 different productions of black tea (not counting productions of the "same" tea across different years).  Within those, there have probably been at least ten meaningful "clusters" in terms of profile.  Some clusters are obvious - while there are perceptible</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvnyxkqherh8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvnyxkqherh8">[6]</a></sup></span><span> differences between different productions of unsmoked lapsang, it is generally extremely obvious that "unsmoked lapsang" is a distinct style, and different instances of it will usually be much closer to each other in terms of flavor profile than they will be to other styles of black tea.</span></p><p><span>It's pretty common for tea enthusiasts to develop a strong and enduring preference for a subset of the broader categories (black</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwcomkcm3vgp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwcomkcm3vgp">[7]</a></sup></span><span>/white/oolong/green/yellow/dark</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbzhbkoe6pg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbzhbkoe6pg">[8]</a></sup></span><span>), and for distinct styles/profiles within those categories, but it's worth exploring pretty widely before settling down.</span></p><h2><span>My Recommended Teas</span></h2><p><span>People's tastes in tea vary wildly.  My recommendations will probably be </span><span>more opinionated than the many others' would be</span><span>; I often like more experimental teas that don't belong to well-recognized existing styles.</span></p><h3><span>Black Tea</span></h3><p><span>I usually like black teas that are on the sweeter side, with malty or fruity profiles.  I usually disprefer floral profiles.  Here are some black teas I've been fond of lately:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/honey-needle" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Honey Needle</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2025-fox-down?variant=47053055754468" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Fox Down</span></a><span> (The Steeping Room also sells this one, but only the </span><a href="https://www.thesteepingroom.com/products/2025-white2tea-fox-down-black-tea" rel="noreferrer"><span>full 200g cake</span></a><span>, not the 25g sample)</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2024-dualist" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Dualist</span></a><span> (sold out, but might come back in a future year)</span></li><li value="4"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2021-sugar-rush?variant=42115235119332" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Sugar Rush</span></a></li></ul><h3><span>White Tea</span></h3><p><span>White tea </span><i><span>used</span></i><span> to be categorized into 4 "grades":</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Baihao Yinzhen (Silver Needle)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Baimudan</span></li><li value="3"><span>Gongmei</span></li><li value="4"><span>Shoumei</span></li></ol><p><span>These grades tracked how early in the season the tea was picked, and thus the ratio of buds to leaves.  Silver Needle is all buds, Shoumei all leaves.  Recently, gongmei was </span><a href="https://old.reddit.com/r/tea/comments/1gku2ov/did_you_know_the_meaning_of_gong_mei_has_changed/" rel="noreferrer"><span>redefined</span></a><span> to refer to a specific heirloom varietal, at least within Fuding.  I have no idea whether gongmei-varietal teas also receive a grade from one of the remaining 3 grades.</span></p><p><span>Silver Needle is the most expensive, but also the most divisive.  I haven't had one that I've liked.  I'm generally not a huge fan of "standard" fresh white teas; I like aged and experimental (and often </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compressed_tea" rel="noreferrer"><span>pressed</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqntqt74rusd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqntqt74rusd">[9]</a></sup></span><span>) whites.  So my recommendations here are not a very good representation of what most people think of when they think of "white tea".</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>PPX 1232</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy0f9qs0kvps"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny0f9qs0kvps">[10]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2014-shoumei-2?variant=31961895895177" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t 2014 Shoumei</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjepsxu2myrt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjepsxu2myrt">[11]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2024-charing-cross?variant=44657927946468" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Charing Cross</span></a></li><li value="4"><a href="https://white2tea.com/products/2019-marion?variant=31961906118793" rel="noreferrer"><span>w2t Marion</span></a></li><li value="5"><a href="https://daxuejiadao.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>dxjd</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftr8bi6qtdg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntr8bi6qtdg">[12]</a></sup></span><span> 2009 Shoumei</span></li></ul><h3><span>Oolong Tea</span></h3><p><span>It was an oolong that got me into this mess.  Alas, but not all first loves last forever: my tastes have changed and I basically no longer drink oolong.  But if you forced me to drink an oolong I'd probably go with a </span><a href="https://yunnansourcing.com/collections/anxi-oolong-tea-tie-guan-yin/products/imperial-aaa-tie-guan-yin-of-anxi-oolong-tea-of-fujian" rel="noreferrer"><span>reasonably high-grade Tie Guan Yin</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflshgknbfie"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlshgknbfie">[13]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><h3><span>Green Tea</span></h3><p><span>A green was my second love.  Unlike oolong, I still like a high-quality </span><a href="https://www.teavivre.com/premium-dragon-well-green-tea.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>longjing</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://www.teavivre.com/bi-luo-chun-green-tea-pi-lo-chun.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>bi luo chun</span></a><span>, but I never find myself reaching for them first.</span></p><h3><span>Yellow Tea</span></h3><p><span>I've only tried one and it really just felt like a slightly weird green.  Maybe there's more to this category but I'm not the one to tell you about it.</span></p><h3><span>Dark Tea</span></h3><p><span>Practically unknown in the west except by tea nerds, dark (fermented) tea is nearly as popular as black (red) tea in China.  (Both are dwarfed by green tea, which accounts for more than half of domestic Chinese tea production and sales.)</span></p><p><span>There's a bunch of subtypes here that I won't get into, since I don't drink any of them (yet).  My few experiences with puerh suggest a wide variety of </span><s><span>dirt</span></s><span> </span><s><span>partially decomposed plant matter</span></s><span> fermented flavor notes, but that might've just been me running into </span><a href="https://white2tea.com/blogs/blog/a-guide-to-types-of-puer-tea-raw-vs-ripe-and-how-to-choose" rel="noreferrer"><span>shou instead of sheng</span></a><span>.  I hope to broaden my horizons one day!</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd59n5zyvix5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd59n5zyvix5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There is a style of brewing and drinking tea called "grandpa", which doesn't use an infuser... and also doesn't remove the tea leaves from the tea. You refill your cup with hot water when you're done.  I don't do this; you're welcome to experiment.  Oolongs, whites, and some puerhs and greens will be best-suited for this - generally aim for teas that are less tannic.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpthqnmxb85e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpthqnmxb85e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I'm partial to the </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Cuisinart-CPK-17-PerfecTemp-1-7-Liter-Stainless/dp/B003KYSLNQ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cuisinart</span></a><span>, but you can get something cheaper, as long as it has options in the 170-180F (green) and 185-195F (oolong, sometimes white) range.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnljkeu94glsb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefljkeu94glsb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Exceptions no doubt exist, but the correlation I've noticed is pretty strong.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrjisk6asgqi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrjisk6asgqi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Remember that brewing a single cup of tea requires 2-3 grams.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8ywoavlb2cc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8ywoavlb2cc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.thesteepingroom.com/collections/white2tea-teas" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Steeping Room</span></a><span> is one example I'm familiar with - they stock a selection of teas from </span><a href="https://white2tea.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span>white2tea</span></a><span>, often with no or minimal markup.  (I assume they buy them in bulk at a discount.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvnyxkqherh8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvnyxkqherh8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To some people.  Often comes with experience.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwcomkcm3vgp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwcomkcm3vgp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Also known as red tea.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbzhbkoe6pg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbzhbkoe6pg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Fermented teas - most famously, puerh.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqntqt74rusd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqntqt74rusd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Generally cakes, not bricks.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny0f9qs0kvps"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy0f9qs0kvps">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This got me into white tea.  Unfortunately, it's only available in China, so you'll either need to scour taobao and risk a couple hundred bucks on a large quantity, or drink tea with me and remind me to break into my stash.  The producer's name is </span><a href="https://pinpinxiang.cn/" rel="noreferrer"><span>pinpinxiang</span></a><span> (品品香).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjepsxu2myrt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjepsxu2myrt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Year specified because it's pretty different from their "generic" shoumei production from subsequent years.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntr8bi6qtdg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftr8bi6qtdg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>They currently seem to not be selling anything, alas.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlshgknbfie"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflshgknbfie">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Many vendors that sell oolongs will sell one or more kind of Tie Guan Yin; Yunnan Sourcing's is decent but they're probably not the best vendor to order from if you just want to try the one thing.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/d4SNYCop34nX2o6ay/how-to-make-good-tea#comments">Discuss</a>
